Step 2: Use the inequality sin(x) ≤ x ≤ tan(x) for x near 0.  
Step 3: Divide all parts by sin(x) (positive near 0): 1 ≤ x/sin(x) ≤ 1/cos(x).  
Step 4: Take reciprocals (reversing inequalities): cos(x) ≤ sin(x)/x ≤ 1.  
Step 5: As x→0, cos(x)→1, so by the squeeze theorem, sin(x)/x→1.  

The limit is **1**.

Step 1: Write the matrix $ A = \begin{bmatrix}1 & 2\\3 & 4\end{bmatrix} $.  
Step 2: Compute the characteristic equation $ \det(A - \lambda I) = 0 $, resulting in $ (1 - \lambda)(4 - \lambda) - 6 = 0 $.  
Step 3: Expand and simplify: $ \lambda^2 - 5\lambda - 2 = 0 $.  
Step 4: Solve using the quadratic formula: $ \lambda = \frac{5 \pm \sqrt{(-5)^2 - 4(1)(-2)}}{2(1)} = \frac{5 \pm \sqrt{33}}{2} $.  
Final answer: The eigenvalues are $ \frac{5 + \sqrt{33}}{2} $ and $ \frac{5 - \sqrt{33}}{2} $.

Status:  complete
Output:  The problem asks for the sum of the series $ \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n^2} $.  

1. **Recall convergence**: The series converges by the p-series test ($ p = 2 > 1 $).  
2. **Use Fourier series**: Consider $ f(x) = x $ on $ [-\pi, \pi] $, whose Fourier series is $ \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n+1} 2}{n} \sin(nx) $.  
3. **Apply Parseval’s identity**: $ \int_{-\pi}^\pi x^2 \, dx = \pi \sum_{n=1}^\infty \left( \frac{2}{n} \right)^2 $.  
4. **Compute integrals**: Left-hand side $ = 2 \cdot \frac{\pi^3}{3} $, right-hand side $ = \pi \cdot 4 \sum \frac{1}{n^2} $.  
5. **Solve for the sum**: $ \frac{2\pi^3}{3} = 4\pi \sum \frac{1}{n^2} \Rightarrow \sum \frac{1}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{6} $.  

The final answer is $ \frac{\pi^2}{6} $.
LLM calls: 1  Latency: 20712ms

1. Recognize $ x^4 - 1 $ as a difference of squares: $ (x^2)^2 - 1^2 = (x^2 - 1)(x^2 + 1) $.  
2. Factor $ x^2 - 1 $ further: $ (x - 1)(x + 1) $.  
3. Factor $ x^2 + 1 $ using complex numbers: $ (x - i)(x + i) $, where $ i = \sqrt{-1} $.  
4. Combine factors: $ (x - 1)(x + 1)(x - i)(x + i) = 0 $.  
5. Set each factor equal to zero: $ x = 1 $, $ x = -1 $, $ x = i $, $ x = -i $.  
These are all four roots of the equation.  

**Final Answer:** The roots are $ 1 $, $ -1 $, $ i $, and $ -i $.

Factoring gives $ (r - 1)(r - 2) = 0 $, so the roots are $ r = 1 $ and $ r = 2 $. Since these are distinct real roots, the general solution is $ y(x) = C_1 e^{x} + C_2 e^{2x} $, where $ C_1 $ and $ C_2 $ are constants.

Status:  complete
Output:  **Problem Statement:**  
Find the derivative of \(f(x) = x^{4} - 2x^{2} + 1\) with respect to \(x\).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**  
The symbolic math engine breaks down this task as follows:  

1. **Derivative of \(x^4\):** Apply the power rule, which states that \(\frac{d}{dx}(x^n) = n x^{n-1}\). This gives us \(4x^3\).

2. **Derivative of \(-2x^2\):** First, factor out the constant coefficient \(-2\), then apply the power rule to \(x^2\):  
   - The derivative of \(x^2\) is \(2x^{1}\).  
   - Multiplying by \(-2\) gives us \(-4x\).

3. **Derivative of the constant term (+1):** Any constant number has a derivative of 0.

Combine these results to get:

\[f'(x) = 4x^3 + (-4x) + 0\]

- Here, \(n = 4\), so the derivative is \(4x^{3}\).

Next, for \(-2x^2\) (note that the constant coefficient remains in the derivative):
- The term can be written as \((-2) \cdot x^2\).
- Applying the power rule to \(x^2\): exponent 2 becomes 1.
- Coefficient: Multiply by the original exponent, so \(-2 \times 2 = -4\).
- Derivative is \(-4x^{1}\), or \(-4x\).

Finally, for the constant term \(+1\):
- The derivative of a constant is zero.

Combining these results:
\[ f'(x) = 4x^3 + (-4x) + 0 = 4x^3 - 4x. \]

**Final Answer:**  
\[ \boxed{f'(x)=4x^{3}-4x} \]

Output:  Okay, let's simplify the expression \(\frac{x^2 - 1}{x - 1}\).

We are simplifying a rational expression where the numerator is \(x^2 - 1\) and the denominator is \(x - 1\). To make it simpler, we'll factor both parts.

First, look at the denominator: It's just \(x - 1\), so no factoring needed here. We can leave this as \(\frac{x^2 - 1}{x - 1}\).

Now, look at the numerator: \(x^2 - 1\). This is a classic "difference of squares" expression.

The difference of squares formula states that \(a^2 - b^2 = (a + b)(a - b)\).

Here, \(a\) is \(x\) and \(b\) is \(1\), so:
\(x^2 - 1^2 = (x + 1)(x - 1)\)

Confirming this: \((x + 1)(x - 1) = x^2 - x + x - 1 = x^2 - 1\). Perfect!

So, the expression becomes:
\(\frac{(x + 1)(x - 1)}{x - 1}\)

Now, we can cancel out the common factor \((x - 1)\) in both numerator and denominator. However, this cancellation is only allowed when \(x \neq 1\) (otherwise division by zero occurs).

After cancelling:
We are left with just \(x + 1\), but remembering that it's undefined at x = 1.

The final simplified form of the expression is therefore \(x + 1\).

**Step 1:** The problem wants two specific tasks for the expression `(x+1)**2`. First, we need to expand it into a polynomial. Second, after expanding, we must factor that resulting polynomial back again.

**Step 2:** Expanding `(x+1)**2` means multiplying the binomial `(x + 1)` by itself.
`(x+1)*(x+1)`

Using the distributive property (FOIL method):
- First term: `x * x = x²`
- Outer terms: `x * 1 = x` and `1 * x = x`
- Inner terms: Same as outer, both are `x`.
- Last terms: `1 * 1 = 1`

Adding these parts together:
**Result:** `x² + 2x + 1`

**Step 3:** Factoring the expanded form means finding factors whose product equals `x² + 2x + 1`. We look for two binomials that multiply to give this expression.
We need two numbers that multiply to `1` (the constant term) and add up to `2` (the coefficient of x). These are `+1` and `+1`.
Also, the leading term is `x²`, so one factor must be `(x + a)`.

Therefore:
**Result:** `(x + 1)(x + 1)`
We can write this as `(x+1)**2`.

The final answer is the expanded form **x² + 2x + 1** and its factored form **(x+1)**2.

*(Step 1): Start with the original form.*
(x + 1)^2

(x + 1) * (x + 1)

Now to factor this *expanded* form, which is x² + 2x + 1:

These factors are: 1 and 1.

*(Step 6): These factors help us rewrite the quadratic part (x² + 2x + ?), but since we already know it's a perfect square, we can use them directly.*
We look for two terms that multiply to x² (so one term is 'x' and the other is 'x').

*(Step 7): The factors of the constant term (1) are used as these multipliers. We need an expression like ('x' + ?) * ('x' + ?).*
Plugging in our factor pair: (x + 1) * (x + 1)

This confirms that factoring x² + 2x + 1 gives:
(x+1)**2 = (x+1)(x+1)

Therefore, the final answer is:
(x+1)**2

Output:  Okay, let's solve this step-by-step.

The task is to find the derivative of the expression 'three times x cubed minus x', and then see if that result can be factored or solved for *x*.

Step 1: Differentiate the given function. The function is f(x) = 3*x**3 - x.
Using the power rule (d/dx [x^n] = n*x^(n-1)), we differentiate each term:
The derivative of 3*x**3 is 9*x**2, and the derivative of -x is -1. So, f'(x) = 9*x**2 - 1.

Step 2: Factor if needed. We can factor this expression.
f'(x) = (3*x)**2 - (1)**2
This fits a difference of squares pattern [a² - b²] which factors to (a + b)*(a - b).
Here, a is 3*x and b is 1.
So, f'(x) = (3*x + 1)*(3*x - 1)

Step 3: Solve for x. We are now asked to solve the equation derived from setting this derivative equal to zero ('f\'(x) = 0').
This gives us:
(3*x + 1)*(3*x - 1) = 0
We know that a product is zero if either factor is zero.
So, we set each factor to zero and solve for *x*:
First: 3*x + 1 = 0 => x = -1/3
Second: 3*x - 1 = 0 => x = 1/3

The final answer is that the derivative is f'(x) = (3*x + 1)*(3*x - 1), and solving it gives *x* equals negative one-third or positive one-third.

Output:  Okay, let's break down what you asked for:

1.  **Expand** (x-2)³: This means multiplying out the expression using algebraic rules.
2.  **Differentiate** the result of that expansion with respect to x: Find its derivative.
3.  **Simplify** the differentiated expression as much as possible.
4.  **Factor** the simplified expression completely, breaking it down into simpler pieces (like binomials) if possible.
5.  **Solve for x=0**: Use the factored form to find the value of x that satisfies the equation set by its derivative being zero.

Here's how I do each step:

1.  To expand (x-2)³, I use the binomial theorem or multiply it out directly.
    *   Using direct multiplication: First, calculate (x-2)*(x-2).
        *   This gives x*x + x*(-2) -2*x -2*(-2), which simplifies to x² - 4x + 4.
    *   Now, I need to multiply this result by the third factor (another x-2): (x² - 4x + 4)*(x-2).
        *   This involves distributing each term in the first expression across the second:
            *   x²*x = x³
            *   x²*(-2) = -2x²
            *   (-4x)*x = -4x²
            *   (-4x)*(-2) = 8x
            *   (4)*x = 4x
            *   (4)*(-2) = -8.
        *   Adding these all up: x³ + (-2x² - 4x²) + (8x + 4x) + (-8).
        *   Combining like terms gives the final expanded form: **x³ - 6x² + 12x - 8**.

2.  Now I differentiate this result with respect to x.
    *   Using the power rule, d/dx(xⁿ)=n*xⁿ⁻¹:
        *   Derivative of x³ is (3)*x³⁻¹ = **3x²**
        *   Derivative of -6x² is (-6)*(2)*x(²⁻1) = **-12x** (since the constant multiple rule applies: d/dx(c*u(x))=c*d/dx(u(x)))

1 = A(0) + B(-2) \implies -2B = 1 \implies B = -\frac{1}{2}
\]

Substitute these values back into the equation. The decomposition is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{x^{2}-1}=\dfrac{\dfrac{1}{2}}{x-1}+\left(-\dfrac{\dfrac{1}{2}}{x+1}\right)}
\]

Simplify for the final answer:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{x^{2}-1}=\dfrac{1}{2(x-1)}-\dfrac{1}{2(x+1)}}
\]

Output:  The problem asks for the derivative of \( e^x \), which is a fundamental exponential function.

To find its derivative, we can use the definition:
\[
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}
\]
For \( f(x) = e^x \):
\[
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^{x+h} - e^x}{h}
\]

Factor out \( e^x \):
\[
= \lim_{h \to 0} \left( e^x \cdot \frac{e^h - 1}{h} \right)
\]

Evaluate the limit as \( h \) approaches 0. Recall that:
\[
\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h} = 1
\]
because this is a standard result from calculus.

Thus, we have:
\[
f'(x) = e^x \cdot 1 = e^x
\]

The derivative of \( e^x \) is itself, so no additional simplification is needed.

**Final Answer:**  
\[ f'(x) = e^x \]

Output:  Okay, let's differentiate the exponential function e^x.

1.  **Restating the problem:**
    We are looking for how much e^x changes when x itself changes slightly. This is exactly what differentiation calculates, giving us the instantaneous rate of change or slope of e^x at any point.

2.  **Derivative Definition (using limit):** To find this formally, we use the definition:
        f'(x) = lim_(h->0) [f(x+h) - f(x)] / h
    For our function f(x) = e^x:
        f'(x) = lim_(h->0) [e^(x+h) - e^x] / h

3.  **Simplifying the expression:**
    We can factor out an e^x from the numerator inside the limit, since it's common to both terms.
        e^(x+h) = e^x * e^h
    So:
        f'(x) = lim_(h->0) [(e^x * e^h - e^x)] / h

4.  **Factoring out e^x:**
        f'(x) = lim_(h->0) [e^x (e^h - 1)] / h
    This can be rewritten as:
        f'(x) = e^x * lim_(h->0) (e^h - 1)/h

5.  **Calculating the limit:**
    The expression `(e^h - 1)/h` is a standard limit used in defining derivatives of exponential functions. As `h` approaches zero, this fraction approaches a specific value.
        lim_(h->0) (e^h - 1)/h = 1
    Therefore:
        f'(x) = e^x * 1

6.  **Final result:**
    The derivative of the function e^x is simply itself.

**The simplified derivative of exp(x) is also exp(x).**

Status:  complete
Output:  Okay, let's tackle this!

We need to find what value sin(x)/x gets closer and closer to as we plug in numbers for x that get really close to 0 (both positive and negative).

First, if I take a value very close to 0, say x = 0.1 radians, then sin(0.1) ≈ 0.09983, so sin(0.1)/0.1 ≈ 0.9983.

Now, even closer: let x be 0.01 radians, sin(0.01) ≈ 0.0099998, so sin(0.01)/0.01 ≈ 0.99998.

Let's try a negative value close to zero first, say x = -0.1, sin(-0.1) ≈ -0.09983, and dividing by -0.1 gives (-0.09983)/(-0.1) ≈ 0.9983.

And for x = -0.01, sin(-0.01) ≈ -0.0099998, divided by -0.01 is approximately 0.99998 again.

So as we get closer and closer to zero from both sides (positive and negative), the value of sin(x)/x seems to approach y = 1.

While this gives us an intuitive feel for the limit, there are other methods like L'Hôpital's rule or Taylor series that can rigorously confirm it if needed. But these calculations strongly suggest what happens right at x=0.

Therefore, as x approaches zero from both sides, sin(x)/x approaches 1.

Output:  Okay, let's simplify sin(x)**2 + cos(x)**2 using a trigonometric identity. The problem asks us to show that adding these two functions squared always gives 1.

We know sine and cosine are ratios in a right triangle related by the Pythagorean theorem. If we consider an angle x (θ) in such a triangle, sin(θ) is opposite over hypotenuse (o/h), cos(θ) is adjacent over hypotenuse (a/h).

Therefore:
sin(x)**2 = (o/h)**2
cos(x)**2 = (a/h)**2

Adding these gives:
(sin(x))² + (cos(x))² = (o/h)² + (a/h)²

Using the Pythagorean theorem, where o² + a² equals h² (the square of the hypotenuse), we can write:
(o² + a²)/h² = h² / h²

Simplifying this fraction results in:
1

So, by definition and the Pythagorean theorem, sin(x)**2 + cos(x)**2 simplifies to 1.

Status:  complete
Output:  Okay, let's find the integral of sin(x) times cos(x) and simplify it.

We are integrating the product of sine and cosine functions with the same argument. We cannot integrate this directly as a simple power rule because we don't have just one trig function raised to a power.

To make it easier, we can use a trigonometric identity that relates sin(2x) to the product of sin(x) and cos(x). The double-angle formula tells us:
sin(2x) = 2 * sin(x) * cos(x)

From this, we can express our original function as half of sin(2x):
sin(x) * cos(x) = (1/2) * sin(2x)

Now the integral becomes much simpler. We are integrating a sine function:
∫ sin(x) * cos(x) dx = ∫ (1/2) * sin(2x) dx

We can factor out the constant 1/2 from the integral:
= (1/2) * ∫ sin(2x) dx

To integrate sin(2x), we treat it as a sine function with argument 'u' = 2x. The derivative of u is du/dx = 2, so dx = du / 2.

Substituting this in:
= (1/2) * ∫ sin(u) * (du / 2)
= (1/2) * (1/2) * ∫ sin(u) du
= (1/4) * (-cos(u)) + C

Now substitute back u = 2x to get the integral in terms of x:
= - (1/4) cos(2x) + C

This is our simplified result, where 'C' represents the constant of integration.

The final answer for ∫ sin(x) * cos(x) dx is (-1/4)cos(2x) + C

Output:  To find the eigenvalues of the given matrix, we solve the characteristic equation. The matrix is:

\[
A = \begin{pmatrix}
1 & 2 \\
3 & 4
\end{pmatrix}
\]

The characteristic equation is derived from det(A - λI) = 0:

\[
A - \lambda I = \begin{pmatrix}
1-\lambda & 2 \\
3 & 4-\lambda
\end{pmatrix}
\]

Compute the determinant of this matrix, which is a quadratic in λ. The result is:

\[
(1-\lambda)(4-\lambda) - (2)(3) = \lambda^2 - 5\lambda - 2
\]

Set to zero and solve using the quadratic formula:

\[
\lambda = \frac{5 \pm \sqrt{(-5)^2 + 8}}{2} = \frac{5 \pm \sqrt{33}}{2}
\]

Thus, the eigenvalues are:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{5 + \sqrt{33}}{2}, \dfrac{5 - \sqrt{33}}{2}}
\]

Output:  Okay, let's find the Laplace transform of \( e^{-2t} \).

**Problem:** We need to compute the integral that defines the Laplace Transform for the function \( f(t) = e^{-2t} \), where \( t > 0 \). This means finding:
\[ L\{e^{-2t}\}(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-2t} \cdot e^{-st}dt \]
for values of s that make this integral converge.

**Steps:**

1. Combine the exponential terms in the integrand.
   This gives:
   \[ L\{e^{-2t}\}(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-(2+s)t}dt \]

2. Evaluate the definite integral \( \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-(2+s)t}dt \).
    This requires finding an antiderivative and applying the limits.
   The antiderivative of \( e^{-kt}\) (where \(k = 2 + s\)) is:
      \[ -\frac{1}{k}e^{-kt} \]

3. Apply the limit as t approaches infinity first to the result from step 2.
    Since Re(s) > -2, this part goes to zero.

4. Evaluate at t=0 for the antiderivative found in step 2 (from step 3).
    This gives:
      \[ -\frac{1}{k}e^{0} = -\frac{1}{k}(1) = -\frac{1}{k} \]

5. Substitute back into the result from step 3 to find L.
    We have \( k = 2 + s \), so:
      \[ L\{e^{-2t}\}(s) = 0 - (-\frac{1}{2+s}) = \frac{1}{2+s} \]
   This holds for Re(s) > -2.

**Answer:**
The Laplace transform of \( e^{-2t} \) is:
\[ L\{e^{-e{-2t}\}(s)\} = \frac{1}{s + 2} \]

**Step 1:** The problem asks for the Laplace Transform of a function: specifically, \( F(s) = \mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\}(s) \), where \( \mathcal{L} \) is defined as \( \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-st} f(t)\, dt \).

**Step 2:** We need to compute the integral: \( F(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-st} \cdot e^{-2t}\, dt \).

**Step 3:** Combine the exponents since they share the base \( e \): \( F(s) = \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-(s+2)t} dt \).

**Step 4:** Evaluate this standard Laplace Transform integral form. The antiderivative of \( e^{-kt} \), for constant \( k \), is \( -\frac{1}{k}e^{-kt} \). Here, \( k = s + 2 \).

**Step 5:** Substitute back into the definite integral: \( F(s) = \left[ -\frac{1}{s+2} e^{-(s+2)t} \right]_{0}^{\infty} \), provided \( s + 2 > 0 \) (which ensures convergence).

**Step 6:** Evaluate the limits. As \( t \to \infty \), and since \( -(s+2)t \to -\infty \) (\( s + 2 > 0 \)), \( e^{-(s+2)t} \to 0 \). At \( t = 0 \), it's \( e^0 = 1 \).

**Step 7:** Calculate the result: The integral is \( F(s) = 0 - \left( -\frac{1}{s+2} \cdot 1 \right) = \frac{1}{s+2} \).

**Step 8:** State the condition for convergence. This solution is valid when the real part of \( s \), Re(\( s \)), is greater than -2 (\( \alpha \)).

The Laplace Transform of \( e^{-2t} \) is therefore:

\( F(s) = \frac{1}{s+2} \)

This result holds for \( s > -2 \).

Status:  complete
Output:  We are solving the ordinary differential equation \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y \) with the initial condition \( y(0) = 1 \).

This equation can be solved by separation of variables. We rearrange it to separate the variables:

\[ \frac{dy}{y} = dx \]

Integrate both sides:

\[ \int \frac{dy}{y} = \int dx \]

The left side integrates as \( \ln|y| + C_1 \) and the right side is \( x + C_2 \). Since constants can be merged, we write:

\[ \ln|y| = x + C \]

Exponentiate to solve for y:

\[ |y| = e^{x+C} = e^C \cdot e^x \]
\[ y = A e^x \]

where \( A = \pm e^C \) is a constant.

Use the initial condition: at \( x=0 \), \( y(0)=1 \):

\[ 1 = A \cdot e^0 \implies A = 1 \]

Thus, the solution is:

\[ y(x) = e^x \]

Verify by differentiating:
\[ \frac{d}{dx} [e^x] = e^x = y(x) \]

and \( y(0)=e^0=1 \), which satisfies the initial condition.

**Final Answer:**
LLM calls: 1  Latency: 19729ms

Status:  complete
Output:  Okay, let's solve this problem together!

We need to find the function y(x) whose derivative is exactly itself everywhere (y'(x) = y(x)), and that also equals 1 at x=0.

Starting with our equation: dy/dx = y. We can rearrange it slightly: (1/y) * dy = dx, assuming y ≠ 0 for now.

We integrate both sides separately:
∫(1/y) dy from ? to y
∫dx from 0 to x

The left side integral is ln|y| + C₁ (where |y| means the absolute value of y).
The right side integral evaluated between 0 and x gives: ∫₀ˣ dx = [x]₀ˣ = x - 0 = x.

So, combining these:
ln|y| + C₁ = x + C₂

Since all constants are arbitrary, we can combine them into one constant (let's call it C):
ln|y| = x + C

Exponentiating both sides to solve for y gives us the general solution form:
e^(ln|y|) = e^(x+C)
Which simplifies to |y| = e^C * e^x, or equivalently: y(x) = ± K * e^x
Where we've renamed our constant as K (and included a possible negative sign).

Now, we use the initial condition y(0)=1. Plugging in x=0 and y=1:
y(0) = ± K * e⁰ => 1 = ± K * 1 => So K must be positive or negative depending on our chosen sign.

To satisfy this with a positive value at zero, let's choose the + sign: y(x) = K * e^x

Then plugging x=0 and y=1:
K * e⁰ = K * 1 = K
But we need this to be equal to 1, so K must be 1.

Therefore, our specific solution is y(x) = 1 * e^x, or simply y(x) = e^x.

Let's double-check: Derivative of e^x is indeed e^x. At x=0, e⁰ equals 1, matching the initial condition. Perfect!

So, the final answer for this ODE with its initial condition is:
y(x) = e^x

Output:  Okay, let's tackle the problem of finding the symbolic sum of 1/n² from n=1 to infinity. Here's how you can understand it:

We want to find out what value you get when you add up infinitely many terms: first term is 1/1² (which is 1), then 1/2², then 1/3², and so on forever.

To solve this symbolically without a computer program, we look at the properties of functions. One clever way involves looking at the function f(x) = x² defined between -π and π. We can represent this function as an infinite sum (its Fourier series). By using Parseval's theorem from Fourier analysis, which relates the energy in the time domain to the frequency domain, we connect the coefficients of this series back to our original problem.

The steps involve calculating specific integrals related to f(x) and its periodic extension over [-π, π], then equating two expressions for the same thing (the total power according to Parseval's theorem). This process leads us directly to the sum Σ(1/n²).

Let me show you how this is done step by step.

First, recall that the Fourier series of a function f(x) on [-π, π] is:
f(x) = Σ[c₀ + Σ(cₙ * cos(nx) + dₙ * sin(nx))]
from n=1 to ∞

For our function f(x)=x² (even function), we only need the cosine coefficients. We calculate c₀ and cₙ for this specific f(x).

We find that:
c₀ = 1/π * [-π]∫[π] x dx / π
Let me compute that integral carefully.
(-2) + (-2)
The integral from -π to π of x dx is (x²)/2 evaluated from -π to π, which gives [(π²)/2] - [((-π)²)/2] = (π²/2) - (π²/2) = 0.

So c₀ becomes:
c₀ = 1/π * π * (-4*1 + ...) wait no.
(-3) + (-3)
The average value over the interval [-π, π] is f(x)dx integrated from -π to π and divided by the length of the interval (2π). So c₀ should be:
c₀ = (1/(2π)) * ∫[-π, π] x² dx

Compute that integral: ∫x² dx from -π to π.
The antiderivative is (x³)/3. Evaluate at π gives π³/3 and at -π gives (-π)³/3 = -π³/3.

So the definite integral is [π³/3] - [-π³/₃] = 2*(π³/3)

Then c₀ = (1/(2π)) * (2*π³/₃) = π²/₃

Okay, that's correct. Now let's find cₙ.

cₙ = (1/π) * ∫[-π, π] x² cos(nx) dx for n ≥ 1.

This integral isn't trivial and requires integration by parts twice:

Let Iₙ = ∫x²cos(nx)dx from -π to π.

We know the function is even so we can just double up the interval [0,π]:
cₙ = (2/π) * ∫[0,π] x² cos(nx) dx

Set u = x² and dv = cos(nx) dx.
Then du = 2x

**1. Restating the problem:**  
We need to find out what the total sum is when we add together fractions like 1/(1*1), then 1/(2*2) (which is 1/4), plus 1/(3*3) (1/9), and keep doing this for all positive integers, going on forever. Each term corresponds to n squared in the denominator.

**2. Showing my working:**  
This sum resembles the famous Basel Problem solved by Euler. He used an infinite product representation of sine: sin(πx)/(πx) = ∏ (1 - x²/n²). Taking the logarithm and expanding using Taylor series leads us to a relationship involving Zeta functions, specifically ζ(s) for even integers.

To find ζ(2), we can use its Fourier series. Since 4 * π * (-x)^3 / 3! + something similar is involved in the Fourier cosine series of x² on [-π, π], evaluating both sides at specific points (like x = π or using limits) connects it to Zeta(2). Specifically, integrating both sides from -π to π gives us a direct link involving ζ(2).

The final result for this integral is derived by solving the equation that relates the sum of 1/n² and the Fourier series evaluation. Careful calculation shows that ζ(2) = ∑ (from n=1 to ∞) 1/n² equals π²/6.

**Final answer:**  
π²/6

Output:  Okay, let's find all the values of 'x' that make the equation \( x^4 - 1 \) equal to zero.

The problem asks for all roots (values of \( x \)) of the polynomial \( x^4 - 1 = 0 \), expressed in their simplest forms. This means solving \( x^4 = 1 \).

Step by step:

1. Notice that \( x^4 - 1 \) is a difference of squares: it equals \( (x^2)^2 - (1)^2 \).
   So, we factor it as:
   $$
   x^4 - 1 = (x^2 - 1)(x^2 + 1)
   $$

2. Now, look at each part separately.

3. For \( x^2 - 1 \), this is also a difference of squares: equals \( (x)^2 - (1)^2 \).
   So, we factor it further as:
   $$
   x^2 - 1 = (x - 1)(x + 1)
   $$

4. For \( x^2 + 1 \), this equation is \( x^2 + 1 = 0 \).
   Rearranging gives: \( x^2 = -1 \).

5. The solution to \( x^2 = -1 \) involves the imaginary unit 'i' (where \( i^2 = -1 \)).
   Taking square roots directly:
   $$
   x = \pm \sqrt{-1} = \pm i
   $$

6. Combining all factors, we have four factors: \( (x - 1) \), \( (x + 1) \), and each of the two solutions from step 5.

7. Therefore, the complete set of roots is:
   $$
   x = \pm 1, \quad x = i, \quad x = -i
   $$

Expressing all four roots:

The roots are \( x = 1 \), \( x = -1 \), \( x = i \), and \( x = -i \).

These are the four solutions (two real, two complex) to the equation \( x^4 - 1 = 0 \).

7.  The general solution is formed by combining exponential solutions corresponding to each distinct root, using arbitrary constants `C_1` and `C_2`: `y(x) = C_1*e^(x) + C_2*e^(2*x)`.

This is a homogeneous linear ordinary differential equation (ODE) with constant coefficients. We assume a solution of the form **y(x) = e^(rx)**, where r is an unknown constant.

1.  The first derivative is y'(x) = re^(rx).
2.  The second derivative is y''(x) = r^2e^(rx).

Now plug these into the original ODE:

3.  (r^2e^(rx)) - 3(re^(rx)) + 2(e^(rx)) = 0

Factor out e^(rx), which is never zero for any real x and complex-valued functions aren't considered here, so we set its coefficient to zero:

4.  r^2 - 3r + 2 = 0
5.  This gives the characteristic equation.

Solve this quadratic equation for r:

6.  (r - 1)(r - 2) = 0

So the roots are **r₁ = 1** and **r₂ = 2**.

Since we have two distinct real roots, the general solution to such an ODE is given by a linear combination of e^(rx) solutions:

7.  y(x) = C₁ * e^((r₁)*x) + C₂ * e^((r₂)*x)

8.  Plugging in our values for r₁ and r₂, the solution is **y(x) = C₁*e^(x) + C₂*e^(2x)**.

This form contains all possible solutions because any linear combination of these two exponential functions will satisfy the given ODE.
